title="Bamberg State Library">Bamberg State Library</a></div></div><div class="tsingle" style="width:142px;max-width:142px"><div class="thumbimage"><a href="/wiki/File:Boleslaus_I.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fe/Boleslaus_I.jpg/140px-Boleslaus_I.jpg" decoding="async" width="140" height="181" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fe/Boleslaus_I.jpg/210px-Boleslaus_I.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fe/Boleslaus_I.jpg/280px-Boleslaus_I.jpg 2x" data-file-width="463" data-file-height="600" /></a></div><div class="thumbcaption"><a href="/wiki/Boles%C5%82aw_I_of_Poland" class="mw-redirect" title="Bolesław I of Poland">Bolesław I of Poland</a> in a drawing by <a href="/wiki/Jan_Matejko" title="Jan Matejko">Jan Matejko</a></div></div></div></div></div>
<p>The untimely death of Emperor Otto III at age 21 in 1002 upset the young Emperor's ambitious <i>renovatio</i> plans, which were never fully implemented. Henry II reversed Otto III's eastern policies,<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> damaging the excellent relationship Germany and Bolesław I of Poland had enjoyed during Otto III's reign. Bolesław I had been a loyal supporter of Otto III, but Henry II's actions caused Bolesław I to seek new German allies. Of the major candidates seeking to succeed to the German throne, Bolesław I supported <a href="/wiki/Eckard_I,_Margrave_of_Meissen" title="Eckard I, Margrave of Meissen">Margrave Eckard I of Meissen</a> over Henry. Only after Eckard was assassinated by Saxon nobles in April 1002 did Bolesław I lend his support to Henry II.<sup id="cite_ref-feld_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-feld-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Bolesław I traveled to <a href="/wiki/Merseburg" title="Merseburg">Merseburg</a> on 25 July 1002 and paid homage to the new German king. Bolesław I had taken advantage of Germany's internal strife following Otto III's death, occupying important German territories west of the <a href="/wiki/Oder" title="Oder">Oder River</a>: the <a href="/wiki/Margraviate_of_Meissen" class="mw-redirect" title="Margraviate of Meissen">March of Meissen</a> and the <a href="/wiki/March_of_Lusatia" title="March of Lusatia">March of Lusatia</a>. Bolesław I took control of these territories following the assassination of Margrave Eckard I. Henry II accepted Bolesław I's gains, allowing the Polish Duke to keep Lusatia as a <a href="/wiki/Fief" title="Fief">fief</a>, with Bolesław I recognizing Henry II as his overlord. Henry II refused to allow Bolesław I to keep possession of Meissen, however. Shortly after Bolesław I's departure from Merseburg, an assassination attempt was made against him. Though the attempt failed, Bolesław I was seriously injured. The Polish Duke accused Henry II of instituting the attack, and relations between the two countries were severed.<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> Bolesław I also refused to pay tribute to Germany.
</p><p>Prior to open rebellion in 1004, <a href="/wiki/Boleslaus_III,_Duke_of_Bohemia" title="Boleslaus III, Duke of Bohemia">Boleslaus III, Duke of Bohemia</a>, was ousted in a revolt in 1002. Bolesław I intervened in the Bohemian affair and reinstalled Boleslaus III upon the Bohemian throne in 1003. Boleslaus III soon undermined his own position, however, by ordering a massacre of his leading nobles. Bohemian nobles secretly sent a messenger to Bolesław I, requesting his direct intervention in the crisis. The Polish duke willingly agreed and invited the Bohemian duke to Poland. There, Boleslaus III was captured, blinded, and imprisoned, where he would remain until his death some thirty years later. Claiming dominion over Bohemia for himself, Bolesław I invaded Bohemia in 1003 and conquered the duchy without any serious opposition. Bohemia had previously been under the influence and protection of Germany, with the Polish invasion further increasing tension between Germany and Poland.
</p><p>Bolesław I openly rebelled against Henry II's rule in 1004, burning down the castle in <a href="/wiki/Meissen" title="Meissen">Meissen</a> in an act of war. Returning from Italy after reclaiming the Italian throne, Henry II launched a military campaign against Poland in 1004 that would last until 1018, spanning three wars and several smaller campaigns.<sup id="cite_ref-Cambridge1995_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Cambridge1995-20">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="First_Polish_War">First Polish War</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Henry_II,_Holy_Roman_Emperor&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: First Polish War">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Returning from his first expedition to Italy, in 1004 Henry II gathered an army to march against Poland. The previous year in 1003, Henry II had formed an alliance with the <a href="/wiki/Pagan" class="mw-redirect" title="Pagan">pagan</a> <a href="/wiki/Slavs" title="Slavs">Slavic</a> <a href="/wiki/Lutici" title="Lutici">Lutici</a> tribe. As a consequence of their military alliance, Henry II halted <a href="/wiki/Christianization" title="Christianization">Christianization</a> efforts among the Slavic peoples. The new alliance with the Western Slavs against Poland was controversial, however. Many German nobles had hoped for continued missionary work and the direct submission of the <a href="/wiki/Elbe_Slavs" class="mw-redirect" title="Elbe Slavs">Elbe Slavs</a>. In addition, many German nobles opposed the war because they had developed family ties with Poland during Otto III's reign. Similarly, members of the clergy, including Bishop <a href="/wiki/Bruno_of_Querfurt" title="Bruno of Querfurt">Bruno of Querfurt</a>, saw future <a href="/wiki/Tithe" title="Tithe">tithe</a> payments and Polish support for missionary work in <a href="/wiki/Prussia" title="Prussia">Prussia</a> evaporating due to the war.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (February 2015)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>In preparation for Henry II's coming military invasion, Bolesław I developed a similar alliance with other Slavic peoples. With his conquest west of the Oder River in 1002, his domain stretched from the <a href="/wiki/Baltic_Sea" title="Baltic Sea">Baltic Sea</a> to the <a href="/wiki/Carpathian_Mountains" title="Carpathian Mountains">Carpathian Mountains</a>. Furthermore, the Polish Duke was connected by kinship to numerous princes of <a href="/wiki/Scandinavia" title="Scandinavia">Scandinavia</a>.
</p><p>Henry II answered Bolesław I's rebellion by invading in the summer of 1004, reaching the <a href="/wiki/Ore_Mountains" title="Ore Mountains">Ore Mountains</a> in northern Bohemia. He then conquered the castle at <a href="/wiki/%C5%BDatec" title="Žatec">Žatec</a> and wiped out the Polish army left there. At the same time, <a href="/wiki/Jarom%C3%ADr,_Duke_of_Bohemia" title="Jaromír, Duke of Bohemia">Jaromir</a> (the younger brother of the deposed Bohemian Duke Boleslaus III) invaded Bohemia with German military support. At Merseburg, Jaromír promised to hold Bohemia as a vassal under Henry II, definitively incorporating Bohemia into the <a href="/wiki/Holy_Roman_Empire" title="Holy Roman Empire">Holy Roman Empire</a>. Forcing Bolesław I to flee, Jaromír occupied <a href="/wiki/Prague" title="Prague">Prague</a> with a German army and proclaimed himself Duke. The state he regained was a small one, however, as Polish forces would hold <a href="/wiki/Moravia" title="Moravia">Moravia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Silesia" title="Silesia">Silesia</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Lusatia" title="Lusatia">Lusatia</a> until 1018.<sup id="cite_ref-feld_18-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-feld-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During the next part of the offensive, Henry II retook Meissen and, in summer 1005, his army advanced deep into Poland, suffering significant losses along the way. At the Polish city of <a href="/wiki/Pozna%C5%84" title="Poznań">Poznań</a>, the German forces were ambushed by the Polish army and suffered significant losses. Meeting in Poznań, Henry II and Bolesław I signed a <a href="/wiki/Peace_treaty" title="Peace treaty">peace treaty</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceC_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceC-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> According to its terms, Bolesław I lost Lusatia and Meissen and was forced to give up his claim to the Bohemian throne. The peace lasted only two years as neither party recognized the claims of the other.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Second_Polish_War">Second Polish War</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Henry_II,_Holy_Roman_Emperor&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Second Polish War">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1007, Henry II denounced the Peace of Poznań, resulting in Bolesław I's attack on the <a href="/wiki/Archbishopric_of_Magdeburg" title="Archbishopric of Magdeburg">Archbishopric of Magdeburg</a> as well as his re-occupation of the marches of Lusatia and Meissen, including the city of <a href="/wiki/Bautzen" title="Bautzen">Bautzen</a>. The German <a href="/wiki/Counteroffensive" class="mw-redirect" title="Counteroffensive">counter-offensive</a> began three years later in 1010. It was of no significant consequence, beyond some pillaging in Silesia. In 1012, a second peace treaty between Germany and Poland was signed. Bolesław I quickly broke the peace, however, and once again invaded Lusatia. Bolesław I's forces pillaged and burned the city of <a href="/wiki/Lubusz" class="mw-redirect" title="Lubusz">Lubusz</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceC_21-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceC-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> In 1013, a third peace treaty was signed at Merseburg, requiring in part that Bolesław I recognize Henry II as his overlord in exchange for receiving the <a href="/wiki/March_of_Lusatia" title="March of Lusatia">March of Lusatia</a> and the <a href="/wiki/March_of_Meissen" class="mw-redirect" title="March of Meissen">March of Meissen</a> as <a href="/wiki/Fief" title="Fief">fiefs</a>. To seal their peace, Bolesław I's son <a href="/wiki/Mieszko_II_Lambert" title="Mieszko II Lambert">Mieszko II</a> married <a href="/wiki/Richeza_of_Lotharingia" title="Richeza of Lotharingia">Richeza of Lotharingia</a>, daughter of the <a href="/wiki/Count_Palatine" class="mw-redirect" title="Count Palatine">Count Palatine</a> <a href="/wiki/Ezzo,_Count_Palatine_of_Lotharingia" title="Ezzo, Count Palatine of Lotharingia">Ezzo</a> of <a href="/wiki/Lotharingia" title="Lotharingia">Lotharingia</a>, granddaughter of <a href="/wiki/Otto_II,_Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Otto II, Holy Roman Emperor">Emperor Otto II</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Cambridge1995_20-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Cambridge1995-20">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Reign_as_emperor">Reign as emperor</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Henry_II,_Holy_Roman_Emperor&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Reign as emperor">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Imperial_coronation">Imperial coronation</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Henry_II,_Holy_Roman_Emperor&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Imperial coronation">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:202px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Henry_II_crowned_Holy_Roman_Emperor.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/24/Henry_II_crowned_Holy_Roman_Emperor.jpg/200px-Henry_II_crowned_Holy_Roman_Emperor.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="191" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/24/Henry_II_crowned_Holy_Roman_Emperor.jpg/300px-Henry_II_crowned_Holy_Roman_Emperor.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/24/Henry_II_crowned_Holy_Roman_Emperor.jpg/400px-Henry_II_crowned_Holy_Roman_Emperor.jpg 2x" data-file-width="509" data-file-height="486" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Henry_II_crowned_Holy_Roman_Emperor.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Henry II crowned as <i><a href="/wiki/Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Holy Roman Emperor">Emperor</a></i> by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Benedict_VIII" title="Pope Benedict VIII">Pope Benedict VIII</a> in 1014.</div></div></div>
<p><a href="/wiki/Pope_John_XVIII" title="Pope John XVIII">John XVIII</a> reigned as pope from 1003 until 1009. He was succeeded by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Sergius_IV" title="Pope Sergius IV">Sergius IV</a> from 1009 to 1012. Both John XVIII and Sergius IV, though the nominal Pope, were subservient to the power <a href="/wiki/John_Crescentius" title="John Crescentius">John Crescentius</a>. As leader of the <a href="/wiki/Crescentii" title="Crescentii">Crescentii</a> clan and <i>Patrician of Rome</i>, John Crescentius was the effective ruler of the city. John Crescentius' influence prevented Henry II from meeting the Pope on numerous occasions, preventing him from claiming the imperial title. Following Sergius IV's death in 1012, <a href="/wiki/Pope_Benedict_VIII" title="Pope Benedict VIII">Benedict VIII</a> was elected to succeed him. Upon assuming the <a href="/wiki/Chair_of_St._Peter" class="mw-redirect" title="Chair of St. Peter">chair of St. Peter</a>, however, Benedict VIII was forced to flee Rome by <a href="/wiki/Antipope_Gregory_VI" title="Antipope Gregory VI">Gregory VI</a>, an <a href="/wiki/Antipope" title="Antipope">antipope</a>, whom John Crescentius installed as the new head of the Catholic Church. Fleeing across the Alps to Germany, Benedict VIII appealed to Henry II for protection. Henry II agreed to restore Benedict VIII to his papal throne in return for his coronation as emperor.
</p><p>Near the end of 1013, Henry II gathered his army at <a href="/wiki/Augsburg" title="Augsburg">Augsburg</a> to march into <a href="/wiki/Italy" title="Italy">Italy</a>. Earlier in 1013, Henry signed a peace treaty with Duke Bolesław of Poland at <a href="/wiki/Merseburg" title="Merseburg">Merseburg</a>. The peace with Poland gave Henry opportunity to address affairs in Italy. On the march across the Alps, Henry was accompanied by his wife, <a href="/wiki/Cunigunde_of_Luxembourg" title="Cunigunde of Luxembourg">Queen Cunigunde</a>, and a number of clerics. Upon reaching <a href="/wiki/Pavia" title="Pavia">Pavia</a> other bishops and abbots joined him. Henry's forces trapped the <a href="/wiki/King_of_Italy" title="King of Italy">King of Italy</a> Arduin in his capital of <a href="/wiki/Ivrea" title="Ivrea">Ivrea</a>, where he remained until 1015.
</p><p>Henry II arrived in <a href="/wiki/Rome" title="Rome">Rome</a> in early 1014, restoring Benedict VIII as pope. On 14 February 1014, the Pope crowned Henry II as <a href="/wiki/Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Holy Roman Emperor">Holy Roman Emperor</a> ("Romanorum Imperator") in <a href="/wiki/Old_St._Peter%27s_Basilica" title="Old St. Peter&#39;s Basilica">St. Peter's Basilica</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Kampers_6-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kampers-6">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> Then, under the presidency of the Emperor and Pope, the two held a <a href="/wiki/Synod" title="Synod">synod</a> in Rome, appointed five bishops, issued decrees against simony and promoting chastity within the clergy, and ordered the restitution of Church property. Shortly afterwards, the Emperor moved north again where he established the <a href="/wiki/Diocese_of_Bobbio" class="mw-redirect" title="Diocese of Bobbio">Diocese of Bobbio</a>. Celebrating <a href="/wiki/Easter" title="Easter">Easter</a> in <a href="/wiki/Pavia" title="Pavia">Pavia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Italy" title="Italy">Italy</a>, Henry then returned to <a href="/wiki/Germany" title="Germany">Germany</a> in mid-May 1014. He left the rule of Rome to the Pope and thereafter rarely intervened in the politics of <a href="/wiki/Italy" title="Italy">Italy</a> or the <a href="/wiki/Papal_States" title="Papal States">Papal States</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1015 the conflict with Arduin came to a close when Arduin became ill and sought peace with Henry II. He resigned the office of <a href="/wiki/Margrave_of_Ivrea" class="mw-redirect" title="Margrave of Ivrea">Margrave of Ivrea</a> to become a <a href="/wiki/Monk" title="Monk">monk</a> in a monastery at <a href="/wiki/Fruttuaria" class="mw-redirect" title="Fruttuaria">Fruttuaria</a>. He died on 14 December 1015. His brief "reign" as <a href="/wiki/King_of_Italy" title="King of Italy">King of Italy</a> would be the last time a native Italian would reign over Italy until its unification under <a href="/wiki/Victor_Emmanuel_II" title="Victor Emmanuel II">Victor Emmanuel II</a> in 1861. After Arduin's death Henry ordered the Margraviate of Ivrea, which had given the Ottonian Emperors so much trouble, dissolved.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Third_Polish_War">Third Polish War</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Henry_II,_Holy_Roman_Emperor&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Third Polish War">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/German-Polish_War_(1002%E2%80%931018)" class="mw-redirect" title="German-Polish War (1002–1018)">German-Polish War (1002–1018)</a> and <a href="/wiki/Peace_of_Bautzen" title="Peace of Bautzen">Peace of Bautzen</a></div>
<p>The peace agreement of 1013 between Henry II and Bolesław I of Poland quickly deteriorated. In 1014, with Henry II absent from Germany, Bolesław I sent his son <a href="/wiki/Mieszko_II_Lambert" title="Mieszko II Lambert">Mieszko II Lambert</a> to the <a href="/wiki/Duchy_of_Bohemia" title="Duchy of Bohemia">Duchy of Bohemia</a> in order to persuade the new Bohemian Duke <a href="/wiki/Old%C5%99ich,_Duke_of_Bohemia" title="Oldřich, Duke of Bohemia">Oldřich</a> into an alliance against Henry II. The mission failed and Oldřich imprisoned Mieszko II. He was released only after the intervention of the Emperor, who, despite the planned invasion of Poland, loyally acted on behalf of his nominal vassal Bolesław I. As a result, Mieszko II was sent to Henry II's imperial court in Merseburg as a hostage. Henry II probably wanted to force the presence of Bolesław I in Merseburg and make him explain his actions. The plan failed, however, because, under pressure from his relatives, the Emperor soon agreed to release Mieszko II.<sup id="cite_ref-Thompson2016_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Thompson2016-23">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At the same time, Henry II entertained <a href="/wiki/Yaroslav_the_Wise" title="Yaroslav the Wise">Yaroslav</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Pretender" title="Pretender">pretender</a> to the throne of the <a href="/wiki/Kievan_Rus%27" title="Kievan Rus&#39;">Kievan Rus'</a>. A son of Kievan Grand Duke <a href="/wiki/Vladimir_the_Great" title="Vladimir the Great">Vladimir the Great</a>, he was vice-regent of the <a href="/wiki/Principality_of_Novgorod" class="mw-redirect" title="Principality of Novgorod">Principality of Novgorod</a> at the time of his father's death in 1015. Yaroslav's eldest surviving brother, <a href="/wiki/Sviatopolk_I_of_Kiev" title="Sviatopolk I of Kiev">Sviatopolk I of Kiev</a>, killed three of his other brothers and seized power in Kiev. Henry II's support of Yaroslav was in direct opposition to not only Sviatopolk but to Bolesław I as well. Years before, Bolesław I had married one of his daughters to Sviatopolk, making the new Kievan Grand Duke a son-in-law to the Polish Duke.
</p><p>Henry II returned to Germany in 1015 after being crowned Emperor by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Benedict_VIII" title="Pope Benedict VIII">Pope Benedict VIII</a> and prepared for a third invasion of Poland. With three armies at his command, the largest contingent since the beginning of the conflict in 1004, the Imperial army simultaneously marched in a <a href="/wiki/Pincer_movement" title="Pincer movement">pincer movement</a> from the German north, south, and center. Henry II himself commanded the center army, supported by allied Slavic tribes, and moved from <a href="/wiki/Magdeburg" title="Magdeburg">Magdeburg</a> to cross the <a href="/wiki/Oder_river" class="mw-redirect" title="Oder river">Oder river</a> into Poland. Henry II was soon joined from the south by Bohemian Duke Oldřich and from the north by <a href="/wiki/Bernard_II,_Duke_of_Saxony" title="Bernard II, Duke of Saxony">Duke Bernard II of Saxony</a>.
</p><p>As the Imperial army crossed the Oder river and marched across Poland, Henry II's forces killed or captured several thousand Poles, including women and children. But the Imperial army suffered heavy losses throughout the campaign. Bolesław I sent a detachment of <a href="/wiki/Moravia" title="Moravia">Moravian</a> <a href="/wiki/Knights" class="mw-redirect" title="Knights">knights</a> under the command of Mieszko II in a diversionary attack against the Empire's <a href="/wiki/Saxon_Eastern_March" title="Saxon Eastern March">Eastern March</a>. The Imperial army retreated from Poland to <a href="/wiki/Merseburg" title="Merseburg">Merseburg</a> in order to address the assault without making any permanent territorial gains east of the Oder River. During the retreat to Germany, <a href="/wiki/Gero_II,_Margrave_of_the_Saxon_Ostmark" title="Gero II, Margrave of the Saxon Ostmark">Gero II</a>, margrave of the Eastern March, was ambushed by Polish forces and killed late in 1015. Following the attack on the Eastern March, Bolesław I's forces took the offensive. Bolesław I sent Mieszko II to besiege <a href="/wiki/Meissen" title="Meissen">Meissen</a> in 1017, then under the command of Mieszko II's brother-in-law <a href="/wiki/Herman_I,_Margrave_of_Meissen" title="Herman I, Margrave of Meissen">Margrave Herman I</a>. His attempt at conquering the city failed, however, and he was forced to retreat back to Poland.<sup id="cite_ref-Thompson2016_23-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Thompson2016-23">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Henry II and Bolesław I then opened peace negotiations and a <a href="/wiki/Ceasefire" title="Ceasefire">ceasefire</a> was declared in summer 1017. As negotiations failed by autumn 1017, Henry II again marched his army into Poland. His army reached <a href="/wiki/G%C5%82og%C3%B3w" title="Głogów">Głogów</a>, where Bolesław I was entrenched, but it was unable to take the city. Henry II then besieged <a href="/wiki/Niemcza" title="Niemcza">Niemcza</a>, but was likewise unable to capture the city. As his army besieged Niemcza, disease brought about from the winter cold devastated the Imperial forces. His attacks unsuccessful, Henry II was forced to retreat back to Merseburg in Germany. With this defeat, Henry II was ready to end the war and begin serious peace negotiations with Bolesław I.
</p><p>On 30 January 1018, Henry II and Bolesław I signed a fourth peace treaty, known as the <a href="/wiki/Peace_of_Bautzen" title="Peace of Bautzen">Peace of Bautzen</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Knefelkamp123_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Knefelkamp123-24">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> The Polish duke was able to keep the contested marches of Lusatia and Meissen on purely nominal terms of <a href="/wiki/Vassal" title="Vassal">vassalage</a>, with Bolesław I recognizing Henry II as his feudal lord.<sup id="cite_ref-cmh_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cmh-25">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> Henry II also promised to support Bolesław I in the Polish ruler's <a href="/wiki/Boleslaw_I%27s_intervention_in_the_Kievan_succession_crisis,_1018" class="mw-redirect" title="Boleslaw I&#39;s intervention in the Kievan succession crisis, 1018">expedition to Kiev</a> to ensure his son-in-law, Sviatopolk, claimed the Kievan throne.<sup id="cite_ref-Jas86_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Jas86-26">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> To seal the peace, Bolesław I, then a widower, reinforced his dynastic bonds with the German nobility by marrying <a href="/wiki/Oda_of_Meissen" title="Oda of Meissen">Oda of Meissen</a>, daughter of the Saxon Margrave Eckard I of Meissen.<sup id="cite_ref-Cambridge1995_20-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Cambridge1995-20">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Conflict_with_Byzantium">Conflict with Byzantium</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Henry_II,_Holy_Roman_Emperor&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Conflict with Byzantium">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Henry II's involvement in Italian politics and his coronation as emperor inevitably brought him into conflict with the <a href="/wiki/Byzantine_Empire" title="Byzantine Empire">Byzantine Empire</a>. In 969, Emperor Otto I entered into an alliance with <a href="/wiki/Byzantine_Emperor" class="mw-redirect" title="Byzantine Emperor">Byzantine Emperor</a> <a href="/wiki/John_I_Tzimiskes" title="John I Tzimiskes">John I Tzimiskes</a> in which both Eastern and Western Empires would jointly-govern <a href="/wiki/Southern_Italy" title="Southern Italy">southern Italy</a>. Otto I's death in 973 and John I's death in 976 caused this alliance to deteriorate. Otto I's successor in the West, his son Emperor Otto II, and John I's successor in the East, his nephew <a href="/wiki/Basil_II" title="Basil II">Basil II</a>, brought the two empires once again into conflict over control of southern Italy.
</p><p>Under Otto I and Otto II, the <a href="/wiki/Lombards" title="Lombards">Lombard</a> leader <a href="/wiki/Pandulf_Ironhead" title="Pandulf Ironhead">Pandulf Ironhead</a> expanded Western imperial control over central and southern Italy. Originally appointed by Otto I as Prince of <a href="/wiki/Duchy_of_Benevento" title="Duchy of Benevento">Benevento</a> and <a href="/wiki/Principality_of_Capua" title="Principality of Capua">Capua</a> in 961, Pandulf waged war against the Byzantines as a loyal lieutenant of Otto II. By 978, Pandulf had incorporated all three of the southern Lombard principalities – Benevento, Capua, and Salerno – into the Holy Roman Empire.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> Pandulf's death in 981, however, weakened Western dominance over the Byzantine Empire in southern Italy.<sup id="cite_ref-Sismondi,_pg._29_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Sismondi,_pg._29-28">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> By 982, the entire area once ruled by Pandulf had collapsed. The Byzantines still claimed sovereignty over the Lombard principalities, and the lack of single leader to prevent their advances into Lombard territory allowed the Byzantines to make inroads further north. While in Byzantine territory, Otto II encountered a large Muslim army brought into the region by <a href="/wiki/Abu%27l-Qasim_Ali_ibn_al-Hasan_al-Kalbi" title="Abu&#39;l-Qasim Ali ibn al-Hasan al-Kalbi">Abu al-Qasim</a>, <a href="/wiki/Emirate_of_Sicily" title="Emirate of Sicily">Emir of Sicily</a>, and was soundly defeated in the ensuing <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Stilo" title="Battle of Stilo">battle of Stilo</a> on 14 July 982. The defeat shifted the balance of power in southern Italy into Byzantine favor. While preparing to counterattack the Byzantine advance, Otto II suddenly died while in Rome, with his infant son Otto III succeeding him. With an infant as ruler and a political crisis to address, the Western Empire was unable to challenge Byzantine dominance. This allowed Basil II to build of his defense forces in preparation for a future Western counterattack.
</p><p>In 1017, aided by <a href="/wiki/Normans" title="Normans">Norman</a> <a href="/wiki/Mercenaries" class="mw-redirect" title="Mercenaries">mercenaries</a>, the Lombard noble <a href="/wiki/Melus_of_Bari" title="Melus of Bari">Melus of Bari</a> led a successful rebellion against Byzantine control of <a href="/wiki/Apulia" title="Apulia">Apulia</a>. The Byzantine Empire struck back in 1018 under <a href="/wiki/Catepan_of_Italy" class="mw-redirect" title="Catepan of Italy">Catepan of Italy</a> <a href="/wiki/Basil_Boioannes" title="Basil Boioannes">Basil Boioannes</a>, delivering a devastating defeat to the joint Lombard-Norman force at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Cannae_(1018)" title="Battle of Cannae (1018)">Battle of Cannae</a>. Melus fled to the <a href="/wiki/Papal_States" title="Papal States">Papal States</a> following the defeat. With the Byzantine successes in southern Italy, Pope Benedict VIII took an unusual step in 1020, traveling north across the Alps into Germany to discusses the state of affairs in southern Italy with the Emperor. Meeting Henry II in <a href="/wiki/Bamberg" title="Bamberg">Bamberg</a>, the Pope was accompanied by a large number of Italian secular and ecclesiastical leaders, including Melus. Henry II granted Melus the empty title <i>Duke of Apulia</i> for his actions against the Byzantines. But Melus died just a few days later, on 23 April 1020. After settling some controversies with the bishops of <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Mainz" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop of Mainz">Mainz</a> and <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_W%C3%BCrzburg" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop of Würzburg">Würzburg</a>, the Pope convinced Henry II to return to Italy for a third campaign to counter the growing power of the Byzantine Empire.
</p><p>In 1022, Henry II set out down the <a href="/wiki/Adriatic" class="mw-redirect" title="Adriatic">Adriatic</a> coast for southern Italy commanding a large force. He sent <a href="/wiki/Pilgrim,_Archbishop_of_Cologne" class="mw-redirect" title="Pilgrim, Archbishop of Cologne">Archbishop Pilgrim of Cologne</a> ahead with a slightly smaller army along the <a href="/wiki/Tyrrhenian_Sea" title="Tyrrhenian Sea">Tyrrhenian</a> littoral with the objective of subjugating the <a href="/wiki/Principality_of_Capua" title="Principality of Capua">Principality of Capua</a>. A third army, smaller still, under the command of <a href="/wiki/Poppo,_Patriarch_of_Aquileia" class="mw-redirect" title="Poppo, Patriarch of Aquileia">Patriarch Poppo of Aquileia</a> went through the <a href="/wiki/Apennine_Mountains" title="Apennine Mountains">Apennines</a> to join Henry II in besieging the Byzantine fortress of <a href="/wiki/Troia_(FG)" class="mw-redirect" title="Troia (FG)">Troia</a>. Though Patriarch Pilgrim captured <a href="/wiki/Pandulf_IV_of_Capua" title="Pandulf IV of Capua">Pandulf IV of Capua</a> and extracted oaths of allegiance from both Capua and the <a href="/wiki/Principality_of_Salerno" title="Principality of Salerno">Principality of Salerno</a>, all three of Henry II's armies failed to take Troia. The Byzantine troops could not be forced into a pitched battle, and Henry II was forced to turn back, his army weakened by diseases and suffering heavy losses. Henry II almost executed the treacherous prince of Capua, but he relented at the last moment at Pilgrim's pleading. Instead, Henry II sent him off to Germany in chains and appointed <a href="/wiki/Pandulf_V_of_Capua" title="Pandulf V of Capua">Pandulf V</a> to replace him as prince of Capua. The expedition ultimately achieved little, and Pandulf IV would be reinstated as Prince of Capua as a Byzantine ally in 1026.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Imperial_Policies">Imperial Policies</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Henry_II,_Holy_Roman_Emperor&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Imperial Policies">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Upon assuming the German throne, Henry II revised many policies of his predecessor, <a href="/wiki/Otto_III,_Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Otto III, Holy Roman Emperor">Emperor Otto III</a>. Whereas Otto III had promoted a policy of "Restoration of the Roman Empire" (<i><a href="/wiki/Renovatio_imperii_Romanorum" title="Renovatio imperii Romanorum">Renovatio imperii Romanorum</a></i>), Henry II sought a policy of "Restoration of the Frankish Kingdom" (<i><a href="/wiki/Renovatio_regni_Francorum" title="Renovatio regni Francorum">Renovatio regni Francorum</a></i>). Compared to the other members of the <a href="/wiki/Ottonian_dynasty" title="Ottonian dynasty">Ottonian dynasty</a>, Henry II spent relatively little time in Italy, only traveling south of the Alps three times during his twenty-two year reign. He was absent from the Italian peninsula for over a decade between his expulsion of <a href="/wiki/Margrave_of_Ivrea" class="mw-redirect" title="Margrave of Ivrea">Margrave</a> <a href="/wiki/Arduin_of_Ivrea" title="Arduin of Ivrea">Arduin of Ivrea</a> in 1004 and his return in 1014 to claim the imperial title, allowing the kingdom to mostly govern itself.
</p><p>Henry II's absence from Italy was primarily due to his continued conflict with Bolesław I Chrobry of Poland. During the reign of Otto III, Bolesław I had been a loyal ally of the Empire. However, the protracted German-Polish wars brought the two nations into open warfare for over sixteen years.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Ecclesiastical_affairs">Ecclesiastical affairs</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Henry_II,_Holy_Roman_Emperor&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Ecclesiastical affairs">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Henry II inherited several unresolved <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">ecclesiastical</a> disputes from his predecessor Otto III. Issues of particular importance were the reestablishment of the <a href="/wiki/Diocese_of_Merseburg" class="mw-redirect" title="Diocese of Merseburg">Diocese of Merseburg</a> and the settlement of the <i><a href="/wiki/Gandersheim_Conflict" title="Gandersheim Conflict">Gandersheim Conflict</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-hein_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-hein-29">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<ul><li>The Diocese of Merseburg had been established by <a href="/wiki/Otto_I,_Holy_Roman_Emperor" class="mw-redirect" title="Otto I, Holy Roman Emperor">Emperor Otto I</a> in 968 to commemorate his victory against the <a href="/wiki/Pagan" class="mw-redirect" title="Pagan">pagan</a> <a href="/wiki/Hungarian_invasions_of_Europe" title="Hungarian invasions of Europe">Hungarians</a> at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Lechfeld" title="Battle of Lechfeld">Battle of Lechfeld</a> in 955. Established to conduct <a href="/wiki/Missionary" title="Missionary">missionary</a> work among the pagan <a href="/wiki/Slavs" title="Slavs">Slavs</a>, the Diocese was all but abandoned in 983 following a <a href="/wiki/Great_Slav_Rising" class="mw-redirect" title="Great Slav Rising">major Slavic revolt</a> against Imperial rule. In 1004, Henry II ordered the reestablishment of the Diocese to resume missionary work among the Slavs and appointed the German chronicler <a href="/wiki/Thietmar_of_Merseburg" title="Thietmar of Merseburg">Thietmar of Merseburg</a> to serve as its <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Merseburg" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop of Merseburg">bishop</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>In 987, during the regency of Otto III's mother <a href="/wiki/Theophanu" title="Theophanu">Theophanu</a>, the Gandersheim Conflict, centering around the jurisdiction of <a href="/wiki/Gandersheim_Abbey" title="Gandersheim Abbey">Gandersheim Abbey</a>, first flared up. Both the <a href="/wiki/Archbishop_of_Mainz" class="mw-redirect" title="Archbishop of Mainz">Archbishop of Mainz</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Hildesheim" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop of Hildesheim">Bishop of Hildesheim</a> claimed authority over the Abbey, including the authority to invest the Abbey's nuns. Otto III and Theophanu's intervention eased the tensions between the parties, but did not permanently settle the issue. Henry II only succeeded in suppressing the argument in 1007, and again in 1021. Still unsolved after his death in 1024, a compromise was pushed at an Imperial synod in 1030 in the presence of his successor Emperor Conrad II. Hildesheim was eventually given jurisdiction.<sup id="cite_ref-Wolfram2010_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Wolfram2010-31">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<p>In May 1017, Empress <a href="/wiki/Cunigunde_of_Luxembourg" title="Cunigunde of Luxembourg">Cunigunde</a> became seriously ill, while staying at the imperial estates in <a href="/wiki/Kaufungen" title="Kaufungen">Kaufungen</a>. Henry II vowed to found a <a href="/wiki/Monastery" title="Monastery">monastery</a> on the site if she recovered. Upon her recovery in 1018, Henry ordered the construction of the <a href="/wiki/Kaufungen_Abbey" title="Kaufungen Abbey">Kaufungen Abbey</a>. After Henry II's death in 1024, Cunigunde retreated to the Abbey, where she remained until her own death in 1040.
</p><p>Sincerely religious, Henry II supported service to the Church (he was <a href="/wiki/Celibate" class="mw-redirect" title="Celibate">celibate</a>) and promoted various monastic reforms. He also strongly enforced <a href="/wiki/Clerical_celibacy" title="Clerical celibacy">clerical celibacy</a>, perhaps partly in order that the public land and offices he granted to clerics would not be devised to heirs. He encouraged the reform of the Church, fostered missionary activity, and made several charitable foundations for the poor.<sup id="cite_ref-SHMI_7-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SHMI-7">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Henry II wished to become a monk, and in virtue of his imperial power he ordered the Abbot of <a href="/wiki/Verdun" title="Verdun">Verdun</a> to accept him in his monastery. Thereupon, the Abbot ordered him, in virtue of the vows he had professed, to continue the administration of the empire. Henry II fulfilled his duties in the spirit of humility and service, being convinced that temporal power was given by God for the good of the people.<sup id="cite_ref-SHMI_7-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SHMI-7">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Henry II succeeded in persuading <a href="/wiki/Pope_Benedict_VIII" title="Pope Benedict VIII">Pope Benedict VIII</a> to include the word "<a href="/wiki/Filioque" title="Filioque">Filioque</a>" in the <a href="/wiki/Nicene_Creed" title="Nicene Creed">Nicene Creed</a>. The addition of the term provided that the <a href="/wiki/Holy_Spirit" title="Holy Spirit">Holy Spirit</a> emanated from both <a href="/wiki/God_the_Father" title="God the Father">God the Father</a> and <a href="/wiki/God_the_Son" title="God the Son">God the Son</a>. Together with the concept of <a href="/wiki/Primacy_of_the_Bishop_of_Rome" class="mw-redirect" title="Primacy of the Bishop of Rome">Papal primacy</a>, dispute over this doctrine was one of the primary causes of the <a href="/wiki/East-West_Schism" class="mw-redirect" title="East-West Schism">Great Schism of the Church</a> in 1054.<sup id="cite_ref-Kasper2006_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kasper2006-32">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-World_History_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-World_History-33">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Empire.E2.80.93Church_relations"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Empire–Church_relations">Empire–Church relations</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Henry_II,_Holy_Roman_Emperor&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Empire–Church relations">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:11th-century_painters_-_Gospel_Book_of_Henry_II_-_WGA15924.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/31/11th-century_painters_-_Gospel_Book_of_Henry_II_-_WGA15924.jpg/220px-11th-century_painters_-_Gospel_Book_of_Henry_II_-_WGA15924.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="262" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/31/11th-century_painters_-_Gospel_Book_of_Henry_II_-_WGA15924.jpg/330px-11th-century_painters_-_Gospel_Book_of_Henry_II_-_WGA15924.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/31/11th-century_painters_-_Gospel_Book_of_Henry_II_-_WGA15924.jpg/440px-11th-century_painters_-_Gospel_Book_of_Henry_II_-_WGA15924.jpg 2x" data-file-width="850" data-file-height="1011" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:11th-century_painters_-_Gospel_Book_of_Henry_II_-_WGA15924.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Gospel Book of Henry II</div></div></div>
<p>The Ottonian dynasty's traditional policy of investing celibate clerics in the secular governance of the empire - the <a href="/wiki/Imperial_Church_System" class="mw-redirect" title="Imperial Church System">Imperial Church System</a> - reached its climax under Henry II. Introduced by Emperor Otto I, the Ottonians appointed and integrated the higher clergy into the imperial administration, seeking to establish a non-hereditary counter-balance to the fiercely independent and powerful German <a href="/wiki/Stem_duchies" class="mw-redirect" title="Stem duchies">Stem duchies</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> The dukes have always preferred <a href="/wiki/Political_particularism" title="Political particularism">political particularism</a>, looking to the interests of their respective duchies above the interests of the Empire as a whole. In an effort to unify the Empire under their leadership, the Ottonians increasingly associated themselves with the Church, claiming "divine right" to rule the Empire and presenting themselves as the <a href="/wiki/Fidei_defensor" class="mw-redirect" title="Fidei defensor">protectors of Christendom</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Fuhrmann1986_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Fuhrmann1986-35">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> A key element of this policy was to grant land and bestow the title of <i><a href="/wiki/Princes_of_the_Holy_Roman_Empire" class="mw-redirect" title="Princes of the Holy Roman Empire">Prince of the Empire</a></i> (<i>Reichsfürst</i>) to appointed bishops and abbots at the expense of the secular nobility. Unlike the dukes, these ecclesiastic figureheads would not be able to pass titles and privileges down a dynastic line. The Ottonian monarchs reserved the right to <a href="/wiki/Investiture" title="Investiture">appointment and investiture</a> of bishops of the empire's <a href="/wiki/Proprietary_church" title="Proprietary church">proprietary churches</a> for themselves and commanded loyalty, but this contradicted <a href="/wiki/Canon_law" title="Canon law">canon law</a>, which demanded absolute dedication to the universal Church.
</p><p>Under Henry II, an increasing number of counties were assigned to secular rulership by bishops. He granted numerous and lavish donations of imperial regalia and land to the monasteries and dioceses of the Empire; in fact, no other Holy Roman sovereign was named as often in the memorial records.<sup id="cite_ref-Borgolte2014_12-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Borgolte2014-12">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> With these extensive donations and the expanded powers of the emperor, the Catholic church gradually lost its autonomy. The imperial monasteries and other clerical institutions became so numerous, donations and secular privileges granted them so regular, that they eventually developed into an imperial bureaucracy. The chronicler <a href="/wiki/Thietmar_of_Merseburg" title="Thietmar of Merseburg">Thietmar of Merseburg</a> states that the cooperation of Henry II and the bishops of the empire was more intense than any other ruler of the <a href="/wiki/Middle_Ages" title="Middle Ages">Middle Ages</a>, as the dividing lines between secular and ecclesiastical affairs were blurred beyond recognition. The clergy increasingly viewed Henry II as their <a href="/wiki/Feudal_lord" class="mw-redirect" title="Feudal lord">feudal lord</a>, particularly with regards to military matters.<sup id="cite_ref-Wangerin2019_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Wangerin2019-36">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> The cleric princes came to make up a large part of Henry II's imperial army. For most of Henry II's campaigns against Poland and the Byzantine Empire, the cleric princes constituted the largest contingent. Henry II thus strengthened his control over the empire through the clergy, while gaining a greater control over the Church's spiritual policies.<sup id="cite_ref-Borgolte2014_12-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Borgolte2014-12">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Diocese_of_Bamberg">Diocese of Bamberg</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Henry_II,_Holy_Roman_Emperor&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Diocese of Bamberg">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Diocese_of_Bamberg" class="mw-redirect" title="Diocese of Bamberg">Diocese of Bamberg</a> and <a href="/wiki/Prince-Bishopric_of_Bamberg" title="Prince-Bishopric of Bamberg">Prince-Bishopric of Bamberg</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Bamberger_Dom_BW_6.JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/03/Bamberger_Dom_BW_6.JPG/220px-Bamberger_Dom_BW_6.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="162" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/03/Bamberger_Dom_BW_6.JPG/330px-Bamberger_Dom_BW_6.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/03/Bamberger_Dom_BW_6.JPG/440px-Bamberger_Dom_BW_6.JPG 2x" data-file-width="3299" data-file-height="2427" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Bamberger_Dom_BW_6.JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Bamberg_Cathedral" title="Bamberg Cathedral">Bamberg Cathedral</a></div></div></div>
<p>In 1003, <a href="/wiki/Henry_of_Schweinfurt" title="Henry of Schweinfurt">Henry of Schweinfurt</a>, <a href="/wiki/March_of_the_Nordgau" class="mw-redirect" title="March of the Nordgau">Margrave of the Nordgau</a> in Bavaria, revolted against Henry II's rule. Henry II had promised to install the margrave as his successor to the Duchy of Bavaria in exchange for supporting his claim to the German crown. Upon assuming the throne, however, Henry II refused to honour his promise. Instead, Henry II deposed Margrave Henry in 1004 and abolished the March outright. To assume secular authority over the March's former territory, in 1007, Henry II announced his desire to establish a new diocese in Germany: the <a href="/wiki/Diocese_of_Bamberg" class="mw-redirect" title="Diocese of Bamberg">Diocese of Bamberg</a>. Growing up in the <a href="/wiki/Duchy_of_Bavaria" title="Duchy of Bavaria">Duchy of Bavaria</a>, Henry II was fond of <a href="/wiki/Bamberg" title="Bamberg">Bamberg</a>, even giving his estates there to his wife <a href="/wiki/Cunigunde_of_Luxembourg" title="Cunigunde of Luxembourg">Cunigunde of Luxembourg</a> as her <a href="/wiki/Dower" title="Dower">dower</a> upon their marriage. Mission work among the Slavs of the region had previously been conducted by the <a href="/wiki/Fulda_monastery" class="mw-redirect" title="Fulda monastery">Imperial Abbey at Fulda</a> as part of the <a href="/wiki/Diocese_of_W%C3%BCrzburg" class="mw-redirect" title="Diocese of Würzburg">Diocese of Würzburg</a>. To establish his diocese, Henry II needed to overcome the considerable resistance of the <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_W%C3%BCrzburg" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop of Würzburg">Bishop of Würzburg</a>, as the new diocese would comprise about one-fourth of the former's territory. Henry II desired the new Diocese to aid in the final conquest of the pagan Slavs in the area around Bamberg.
</p><p>Henry II held a <a href="/wiki/Synod" title="Synod">synod</a> in <a href="/wiki/Frankfurt" title="Frankfurt">Frankfurt</a> on 1 November 1007, to build consensus among the bishops of the Empire on the establishment of the Diocese of Bamberg. The Bishop of Würzburg, who hoped that the loss of territory from the formation of the new Diocese would result in his elevation to the rank of Archbishop, was not in attendance. Henry II also assigned a portion of the territory from the <a href="/wiki/Diocese_of_Eichst%C3%A4tt" class="mw-redirect" title="Diocese of Eichstätt">Diocese of Eichstätt</a> to his planned Diocese. At the synod, Henry II obtained permission for the foundation of the Diocese. It was also decided that Eberhard, Henry II's Imperial Chancellor, would be ordained by <a href="/wiki/Willigis" title="Willigis">Willigis</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Archbishop_of_Mainz" class="mw-redirect" title="Archbishop of Mainz">Archbishop of Mainz</a> and <a href="/wiki/Primate_of_Germany" class="mw-redirect" title="Primate of Germany">Primate of Germany</a>, to head the new Diocese. Henry II made many wide-ranging gifts to the new Diocese to ensure its solid foundation. Henry II assigned many counties in the <a href="/wiki/Duchy_of_Franconia" title="Duchy of Franconia">Duchy of Franconia</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Duchy_of_Saxony" title="Duchy of Saxony">Duchy of Saxony</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Duchy_of_Carinthia" title="Duchy of Carinthia">Duchy of Carinthia</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/Duchy_of_Swabia" title="Duchy of Swabia">Duchy of Swabia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Cambridge1995_20-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Cambridge1995-20">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Policy_towards_the_nobility">Policy towards the nobility</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Henry_II,_Holy_Roman_Emperor&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Policy towards the nobility">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>As under his predecessors Otto II and Otto III, the various German dukes of the Empire grew increasingly independent from Henry II. A "German" identity had begun to develop. South of the Alps, Italy also saw the various regional lords grow independent. Increasingly, the Empire's duchies were becoming personal possessions of their respective ducal families as opposed to component parts of the Empire.
</p><p>Henry II's policy towards the nobles was focused on overcoming these family structures within the duchies in order to restore imperial dominance. Henry II, as well as the other Ottonians, relied upon his connection with the Church to justify his power over the dukes. However, unlike under Otto I and Otto II, the various German dukes were no longer bound to Henry II by close family ties. While the <a href="/wiki/Duchy_of_Franconia" title="Duchy of Franconia">Duchy of Franconia</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Duchy_of_Saxony" title="Duchy of Saxony">Duchy of Saxony</a> formed the core imperial support, the <a href="/wiki/Duchy_of_Swabia" title="Duchy of Swabia">Duchy of Swabia</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Duchy_of_Bavaria" title="Duchy of Bavaria">Duchy of Bavaria</a> had grown increasingly rebellious.
</p><p>Unlike his predecessors, Henry II was unwilling to show clemency to those dukes who had rebelled against his authority. This caused a sharp rise in conflict with the secular nobility, which forced Henry II to reinforce the position the clergy enjoyed in the governance of the Empire. It was only through the support of the clergy that Henry II survived the numerous noble revolts against his rule during the first decade of his reign. Even his relatives, such as his brothers-in-law <a href="/wiki/Henry_V,_Duke_of_Bavaria" title="Henry V, Duke of Bavaria">Duke Henry V of Bavaria</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Frederick_of_Luxembourg" title="Frederick of Luxembourg">Count Frederick of Moselle</a>, rebelled. As a result, Henry II systematically reduced the internal power structures of the Bavarian and Swabian dukes. Henry II's lack of sensitivity to the secular nobility also resulted in the <a href="/wiki/German-Polish_War_(1002%E2%80%931018)" class="mw-redirect" title="German-Polish War (1002–1018)">series of wars against Poland</a>. Under Otto III, Polish Duke Bolesław I Chrobry had been viewed as a valued ally; Henry II, however, saw him only as a subject.
</p><p>In 1019, the once loyal <a href="/wiki/Bernard_II,_Duke_of_Saxony" title="Bernard II, Duke of Saxony">Duke Bernard II of Saxony</a>, grandson of Emperor Otto I's trusted lieutenant <a href="/wiki/Hermann_Billung" title="Hermann Billung">Hermann Billung</a>, rebelled against Henry II, having grown frustrated at his lack of respect for the secular nobility.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Death_and_successor">Death and successor</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Henry_II,_Holy_Roman_Emperor&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Death and successor">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:BambergDom-GrabHeinrichKunigunde.JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0c/BambergDom-GrabHeinrichKunigunde.JPG/220px-BambergDom-GrabHeinrichKunigunde.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="158" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0c/BambergDom-GrabHeinrichKunigunde.JPG/330px-BambergDom-GrabHeinrichKunigunde.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0c/BambergDom-GrabHeinrichKunigunde.JPG/440px-BambergDom-GrabHeinrichKunigunde.JPG 2x" data-file-width="2695" data-file-height="1940" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:BambergDom-GrabHeinrichKunigunde.JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>tomb of Henry and Cunigunde by <a href="/wiki/Tilman_Riemenschneider" title="Tilman Riemenschneider">Tilman Riemenschneider</a></div></div></div>
<p>Returning to <a href="/wiki/Magdeburg" title="Magdeburg">Magdeburg</a>, <a href="/wiki/Germany" title="Germany">Germany</a> from southern <a href="/wiki/Italy" title="Italy">Italy</a> to celebrate <a href="/wiki/Easter" title="Easter">Easter</a>, Henry II fell ill in <a href="/wiki/Bamberg" title="Bamberg">Bamberg</a>. After celebrating Easter, Henry retired to his imperial palace in <a href="/wiki/G%C3%B6ttingen" title="Göttingen">Göttingen</a>. He died there on 13 July 1024 at the age of 51, after suffering from a chronic, painful urinary infection. Henry had been working with the Pope to convene a <a href="/wiki/Church_council" class="mw-redirect" title="Church council">Church council</a> to confirm his new system of imperial-ecclesiastical relations before he died, leaving this effort unfinished.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Cunigunde_of_Luxembourg" title="Cunigunde of Luxembourg">Empress Cunigunde</a> arranged for Henry to be interred at <a href="/wiki/Bamberg_Cathedral" title="Bamberg Cathedral">Bamberg Cathedral</a>. Though he left the Empire without significant problems, Henry II also left the Empire without an heir. Some speculate that both he and Cunigunde had taken mutual vows of <a href="/wiki/Chastity" title="Chastity">chastity</a>, because of their piety and the fact that they had no royal issue, but this is disputed.  Their marriage being childless, the Saxon dynasty of the Ottonians died with Henry.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-38">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In early September 1024 the German nobles gathered in <a href="/wiki/Oppenheim" title="Oppenheim">Kamba</a> and began negotiations for selecting a new king. The nobles elected the <a href="/wiki/Franconia" title="Franconia">Franconian</a> noble <a href="/wiki/Conrad_II,_Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Conrad II, Holy Roman Emperor">Conrad II</a> as Henry II's successor, who became the first member of the <a href="/wiki/Salian_dynasty" title="Salian dynasty">Salian dynasty</a>.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Canonization_and_veneration">Canonization and veneration</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Henry_II,_Holy_Roman_Emperor&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Canonization and veneration">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Heinrich_II_und_Kunigunde.JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/32/Heinrich_II_und_Kunigunde.JPG/220px-Heinrich_II_und_Kunigunde.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="290" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/32/Heinrich_II_und_Kunigunde.JPG/330px-Heinrich_II_und_Kunigunde.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/3/32/Heinrich_II_und_Kunigunde.JPG 2x" data-file-width="430" data-file-height="567" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Heinrich_II_und_Kunigunde.JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The Imperial saintly couple, Saint Henry II and <a href="/wiki/Saint_Cunigunde_of_Luxembourg" class="mw-redirect" title="Saint Cunigunde of Luxembourg">Saint Cunigunde of Luxembourg</a>, as depicted in 15th century</div></div></div>
<p>Henry II was <a href="/wiki/Canonized" class="mw-redirect" title="Canonized">canonized</a> in July 1147 by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Eugenius_III" class="mw-redirect" title="Pope Eugenius III">Pope Eugenius III</a>; his spouse, <a href="/wiki/Cunigunde_of_Luxembourg" title="Cunigunde of Luxembourg">Cunigunde</a> was canonized on 29 March 1200 by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Innocent_III" title="Pope Innocent III">Pope Innocent III</a>. His relics were carried on campaigns against heretics in the 1160s. He is the <a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">patron saint</a> of the city of <a href="/wiki/Basel" title="Basel">Basel</a>, <a href="/wiki/Switzerland" title="Switzerland">Switzerland</a>, and of <a href="/wiki/St_Henry%27s_Marist_Brothers%27_College" class="mw-redirect" title="St Henry&#39;s Marist Brothers&#39; College">St Henry's Marist Brothers' College</a> in <a href="/wiki/Durban" title="Durban">Durban</a>, <a href="/wiki/South_Africa" title="South Africa">South Africa</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-schn_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-schn-39">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-hein_29-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-hein-29">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Henry's name, which does not appear in the <a href="/wiki/Tridentine_Calendar" class="mw-redirect" title="Tridentine Calendar">Tridentine Calendar</a>, was inserted in 1631 in the Roman Calendar as a <a href="/wiki/Commemoration_(liturgy)" title="Commemoration (liturgy)">commemoration</a> within the celebration of <a href="/wiki/Pope_Anacletus" title="Pope Anacletus">Saint Anacletus</a> on 13 July, the day of his death and the traditional day for his celebration on a local level. In 1668, it was moved to 15 July for celebration as a <a href="/wiki/Ranking_of_liturgical_days_in_the_Roman_Rite" title="Ranking of liturgical days in the Roman Rite">Semidouble</a>. This rank was changed by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_XII" title="Pope Pius XII">Pope Pius XII</a> in 1955 to that of <a href="/wiki/Ranking_of_liturgical_days_in_the_Roman_Rite" title="Ranking of liturgical days in the Roman Rite">Simple</a>, and by <a href="/wiki/Pope_John_XXIII" title="Pope John XXIII">Pope John XXIII</a> in 1960 to that of <a href="/wiki/Ranking_of_liturgical_days_in_the_Roman_Rite" title="Ranking of liturgical days in the Roman Rite">Third-Class Feast</a>. In 1969, it was returned to its original date of 13 July as an optional <a href="/wiki/Memorial_(liturgy)" title="Memorial (liturgy)">Memorial</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During his lifetime, Henry II became an <a href="/wiki/Oblate" title="Oblate">oblate</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Order_of_St._Benedict" class="mw-redirect" title="Order of St. Benedict">Benedictine Order</a>,<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (December 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> and today is venerated within the Order as the patron saint of all oblates, along with St. <a href="/wiki/Frances_of_Rome" title="Frances of Rome">Frances of Rome</a>.
</p><p>Some online sources suggest that Henry is <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_liturgical_calendar" title="Eastern Orthodox liturgical calendar">venerated</a> as a <a href="/wiki/Confessor_of_the_Faith" title="Confessor of the Faith">Confessor</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Eastern Orthodox Church</a> (either on <a href="/wiki/July_13_(Eastern_Orthodox_liturgics)" title="July 13 (Eastern Orthodox liturgics)">13 July</a><sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> or <a href="/wiki/July_15_(Eastern_Orthodox_liturgics)" title="July 15 (Eastern Orthodox liturgics)">15 July</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> However, Henry II does not appear in any Orthodox <a href="/wiki/Synaxarium" title="Synaxarium">synaxaria</a>, and was only canonised in the West almost a century after the <a href="/wiki/East%E2%80%93West_Schism" title="East–West Schism">East–West Schism</a>; indeed, he is perhaps best known in most Orthodox circles for his introduction of the <i>filioque</i> to Rome.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Family">Family</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Henry_II,_Holy_Roman_Emperor&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Family">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Ottonian_dynasty" title="Ottonian dynasty">Ottonian dynasty</a></div>
<p>Henry II was a member of the <a href="/wiki/Ottonian_dynasty" title="Ottonian dynasty">Ottonian dynasty</a> of kings and emperors who ruled the Holy Roman Empire (previously Germany) from 919 to 1024. In relation to the other members of his dynasty, Henry II was the great-grandson of <a href="/wiki/Henry_the_Fowler" title="Henry the Fowler">Henry I</a>, great-nephew of <a href="/wiki/Otto_I,_Holy_Roman_Emperor" class="mw-redirect" title="Otto I, Holy Roman Emperor">Otto I</a>, first-cousin once removed of <a href="/wiki/Otto_II,_Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Otto II, Holy Roman Emperor">Otto II</a>, and a second-cousin to <a href="/wiki/Otto_III,_Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Otto III, Holy Roman Emperor">Otto III</a>.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Sources">Sources</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Henry_II,_Holy_Roman_Emperor&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Sources">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:202px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Page_of_Thietmars_Chronicle.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/68/Page_of_Thietmars_Chronicle.jpg/200px-Page_of_Thietmars_Chronicle.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="247" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/68/Page_of_Thietmars_Chronicle.jpg/300px-Page_of_Thietmars_Chronicle.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/68/Page_of_Thietmars_Chronicle.jpg/400px-Page_of_Thietmars_Chronicle.jpg 2x" data-file-width="486" data-file-height="600" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Page_of_Thietmars_Chronicle.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Page of Thietmar's Chronicle</div></div></div>
<p>Between 1012 and 1018 <a href="/wiki/Thietmar_of_Merseburg" title="Thietmar of Merseburg">Thietmar of Merseburg</a> wrote a <i>Chronicon</i>, or <i>Chronicle</i>, in eight books, which deals with the period between 908 and 1018. For the earlier part he used <a href="/wiki/Widukind_of_Corvey" title="Widukind of Corvey">Widukind</a>'s <i>Res gestae Saxonicae</i>, the <i>Annales Quedlinburgenses</i> and other sources; the latter part is the result of personal knowledge. The chronicle is nevertheless an excellent authority for the history of <a href="/wiki/Saxony" title="Saxony">Saxony</a> during the reigns of the emperors <a href="/wiki/Otto_III,_Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Otto III, Holy Roman Emperor">Otto III</a> and Henry II. No kind of information is excluded, but the fullest details refer to the bishopric of Merseburg, and to the wars against the <a href="/wiki/Wends" title="Wends">Wends</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Poles" title="Poles">Poles</a>.
</p>
